# Review notes for every encyclopedia item

Generated by `tools/review-notes.py` from the `review` field of each item. Status `draft` means
the item was written from the listed sources and still needs an expert check.

## Land, geology and climate (8)

- **Timor-Leste at a glance: location, size and borders** (`tl.land.overview`, draft, 4 sources): Area differs by source: 14,874 km² (CIA World Factbook, MAF fact file), 14,950 km² (Wikipedia infobox, ASEAN Energy), 15,007 km² (older Factbook editions, Australian fact sheet); the text explains this. Border 253 km (Factbook 2022, Wikipedia); older Factbook editions give 228 km. Ataúro 'about 140 km²' and 'about 25 km north of Dili' come from earlier Lafa data (GlobalSecurity gives 144 km²) – check. Check the 2005 provisional land-border agreement wording, the Ataúro municipality year (2022) and West Timor's time zone (UTC+8). Tetun terms to check: 'Sudeste Aziátiku', 'Illa Sunda Ki'ik', 'Timór Osidentál', 'folla informasaun', 'foho ki'ik' (hills). The shared web-search budget ran out during research, so some details rest on earlier Lafa data and standard references.
- **How the island of Timor was formed** (`tl.land.island_formation`, draft, 5 sources): Timing of the collision differs between studies (onset roughly 10 to 3 million years ago; one study: Timor emerged about 3 million years ago); the text only says 'in the last few million years'. Uplift rates of 0.2–1.5 mm/yr come from conference abstracts (GSA 2004, AGU 2014) whose titles are given descriptively. Check the volcanic line wording (Flores, Alor, Wetar, Ataúro) and that Ataúro's volcanoes are extinct. 'As fast as fingernails grow' is a teaching comparison. Tetun terms to check: plaka tektónika, foho-ahi (volcano), Arku Banda, ahu-ruin (coral), kamada (layer), nabeen (melt), liman-kukun (fingernail), degrau (terrace step).
- **Rocks, fossils and minerals** (`tl.land.geology`, draft, 5 sources): Permian and Triassic fossil details follow the summary of Charlton et al., 'The Permian of Timor' (ANU portal); blastoids are named from general knowledge – check. 'No dinosaur fossils reported from Timor' rests on the absence of any report (no source says it directly). Stegodon timorensis finds are mostly from West Timor (e.g. Atambua); the text says 'on Timor'. Giant rats (Coryphomys) from cave sites in Timor-Leste – check. Natural oil and gas seeps on land – check against the geological synopsis (La'o Hamutuk). Marble 'can be cut and polished' (marble is listed by the CIA). Tetun terms to check: fatuk-ahu (limestone), mármore, osan-mean (gold), mina-rai (petroleum), Pérmiku/Triásiku, krinoide, rai-kuak boot (sinkhole), bua-malus (betel).
- **Mountains: Tatamailau (Ramelau), Matebian, Kablaki and Mundo Perdido** (`tl.land.mountains`, draft, 6 sources): Heights differ by source: Tatamailau 2,963 m (Britannica, current Wikipedia), 2,967 m (timorleste.tl), 2,986 m (older Wikipedia); Matebian 2,372 m (Wikipedia), 2,376 m (timorleste.tl), 2,315–2,316 m (older sources); Kablaki 2,062 m (timorleste.tl) vs 2,459 m (trip reports); Mundo Perdido 1,763 m (summits.com) vs 1,775 m. Manucoco 'almost 1,000 m' – check the exact height. Matebian is placed in Baucau (Wikipedia); check the All Souls' Day pilgrimage and the 1978 siege wording against the Chega! report (sensitive history). Tetun spellings Hatubuiliku and Manukoku follow INL rules – check local usage; 'Mundo Perdido' is kept in Portuguese.
- **Rivers and lakes: Laclo, Lóis, Seiçal, Ira Lalaro, Tasitolu** (`tl.land.rivers_lakes`, draft, 5 sources): River details come from general references and earlier Lafa data (the web-search budget ran out): Laclo 'often called the longest' (no length given), Laclo mouth near Manatuto, Lóis mouth in Liquiçá west of Maubara, Seiçal in Baucau, Laclo do Sul and Clere on the south coast – check. Ira Lalaro: drainage through a sinkhole (Irasiquero stream) and the rare turtle (Chelodina mccordi timorensis) – check. Papal Masses at Tasitolu: 12 Oct 1989 and 10 Sep 2024. Be Malae IBA near Batugade (BirdLife). Tetun river names (Laklo, Seisal, Klere, Komoro, Laklo Sul) follow INL spelling – check local usage; 'Tasitolu' explained as 'tasi tolu'.
- **Seas and coasts: Ombai–Wetar Strait, Savu Sea, Timor Sea** (`tl.land.seas_coasts`, draft, 5 sources): Depth 'more than 3,000 m in places' for the Ombai–Wetar Strait comes from earlier Lafa data and general references – check. Timor Trough and Weber Deep from general knowledge. 'Oé-Cusse coast looks out over the Savu Sea' – check (the enclave lies where the Ombai Strait meets the Savu Sea). Treaty dates: signed 6 Mar 2018, in force 30 Aug 2019. Tetun terms to check: Fossa Timór, kanál kle'an, bee-sulin Indonézia nian (Indonesian Throughflow), du'ut-tasi (seagrass), laloran (waves), anin-boot (storm).
- **Climate and seasons** (`tl.land.climate`, draft, 6 sources): Wet-season months vary by source (Britannica: west monsoon December–March); the text says 'about December to April'. The south coast's second, smaller rainy season (about May–July) comes from general references and earlier Lafa data – check. Rainfall 'about 1,500 mm (58 in)' is Britannica's island-wide average. Coastal daytime ~30 °C and 6 °C per 1,000 m are general approximations. 2015–2016 El Niño: WFP says more than a third of households were affected. 'Often six agro-climatic zones' from the Cambridge chapter abstract. Hungry season about November–February (The New Humanitarian). Tetun terms to check: tempu udan / tempu bailoron, tempu hamlaha, monsaun, kolleita, zona agro-klimátika, Kom (Com).
- **Natural hazards: earthquakes, floods, landslides and droughts** (`tl.land.hazards`, draft, 6 sources): Seroja: UN figures from April 2021 give at least 42 deaths and about 9,779 people displaced in Dili (OCHA, UNFPA); earlier counts were lower (27 on 7 April) and final totals were not checked. The Prime Minister's '40 years' remark comes from news summaries. 2015–2016 drought: WFP (more than a third of households; about 3 in 10 people short of food). 2023–2024 drought: 12 of 14 municipalities (WFP/FAO, via WFP and ACAPS). Civil protection and CVTL roles are described generally. Tetun terms to check: rai-nakdoko, rai-halai (landslide), inundasaun, seka (drought), siklone, derrepente, pelumenus, Nasoins Unidas, valeta, rai-lakan.

## Nature and biodiversity (14)

- **Biodiversity of Timor (Wallacea)** (`tl.nature.biodiversity`, draft, 7 sources): Hotspot figures (about 1,500 endemic plants, 274 endemic birds) are CEPF numbers for the whole Wallacea hotspot, repeated in the 2016–2021 portfolio overviews. Ice-age sea level about 120 m lower is general knowledge (one source gives 70–100 m for some lowstands). 'Reptiles and amphibians that may be new to science' is a general statement – check recent herpetological surveys. Seventh National Report: UNDP news of 29 July 2026; ministry name as given there. Tetun terms to check: hotspot, endémiku, Liña Wallace / Liña Lydekker, ekorejiaun, Área Manu Endémiku, Relatóriu Nasionál Dahitu / Dahaat, aban-bainrua, Fernaun Magalhaens.
- **Birds and endemic species** (`tl.nature.birds`, draft, 6 sources): Species totals differ: 289 on the Wikipedia list (includes introduced and accidental species) vs about 261 in the field guide by Trainor, Coates and Bishop (check its publication year). EBA 164: 35 restricted-range species, 23 endemic; species endemic to Timor island alone are 7 (BirdLife factsheet) or 8 (Fatbirder), so the text says 'a few'. IBAs: 16 (BirdLife, 2007), about 2,910 km² ≈ 20% of the land (BirdLife country profile). Check current IUCN status: Timor green pigeon and Timor imperial pigeon (Endangered), yellow-crested cockatoo (Critically Endangered). Buettikoferella is now often placed in Cincloramphus. Portuguese/Tetun bird names are descriptive (scientific names added) – check local names.
- **Marine life and the Coral Triangle** (`tl.nature.marine`, draft, 8 sources): Ataúro 2016 survey (Conservation International Rapid Assessment, July 2016, 10 sites): average 253 species per site, previous record 216 (Raja Ampat), richest site 315, total about 642–643 species (Mongabay, Reef Builders); a 2012 national survey reported 294 species at one Ataúro site. Cetaceans: Charles Darwin University study 2016–2023, at least 21 species (one other source says 24 for the strait); ANU: more than 2,700 blue-whale sightings since 2014; whale season about October–December (tourism sources). Coral Triangle 'about three-quarters (76%) of known coral species' – general reference. Turtles: 'rare and need protection' (legal status not checked). Tetun terms to check: kima (giant clam), tubaraun, mergulladór, baleia kaxalote, baleia ulun-melaun, boek, kadiuk (crab), bubu (fish trap), ahu-ruin sai mutin (bleaching).
- **The saltwater crocodile (lafaek)** (`tl.nature.crocodile`, draft, 4 sources): Respectful name: the earlier Lafa culture article uses 'avó' (grandparent); the brief asks for 'Avô Lafaek'. Check the preferred Tetun form (avó / abó) and whether 'Avô Lafaek' is the usual phrase; research also mentions the 'Lafaek Diak' (Good Crocodile) creation myth and the crocodile's lulik status. Legal protection since 2000 comes from an idw press release (probably UNTAET Regulation 2000/19) – check. Attack figures: Lakehead University study summary for 2007–2021 (84% male, 73% fishers, 43% under 20, about 70% fatal); another study (130 attacks 1996–2014, 52% fatal) shows a sharp rise after 2007. 'Crocodiles from Australia' is a hypothesis. Quiz 1 asks for the meaning of lafaek in each reader's language (in Tetun, the English word). Lafa app named after the crocodile legend (as in earlier Lafa data).
- **Land animals** (`tl.nature.animals`, draft, 6 sources): Origins of introduced mammals (deer from Java/Bali, macaque from the west, cuscus from the Moluccas, palm civet) follow general references and earlier Lafa text – check against recent archaeology (arrival dates). Timor monitor (Varanus timorensis) on Timor – check. Timor ponies shipped to Australia in the 19th century (Wikipedia 'Timor pony'). Buffalo trampling flooded rice fields is a traditional practice in some areas – check. The Tetun option for 'deer' in quiz 2 is a description. Tetun terms to check: laku (palm civet), lagartu, varanu Timór, mate-rate (funeral), sama (trample), ai-oan, dikur (horn), koelu, busa.
- **Forests and plants: sandalwood, mangroves and more** (`tl.nature.forests_plants`, draft, 5 sources): Sandalwood history is summarised here (details belong to tl.history.sandalwood_trade); the current legal status of cutting and export was not checked. Eucalyptus alba and E. urophylla native to Timor (Wikipedia). Mangrove sites (Metinaro, Hera) and 'most rural families cook with firewood' are general statements – check against recent surveys/census. Lore forest is an Important Bird Area inside Nino Konis Santana National Park. Tetun plant names to check: ai-kameli, ai-parapa, ai-bubur, akadiru (palmyra), kami, sukaer, au, nu, haas, ai-moruk, malus/bua, matan-dook, ai-klaran (heartwood), luhu (basket), tua (palm wine). Portuguese: 'noz-de-vela' for candlenut.
- **Protected areas and Nino Konis Santana National Park** (`tl.nature.protected_areas`, draft, 8 sources): Declaration date: Council of Ministers approval on 27 July 2007 (Kyodo, via ETAN); other sources give 3 or 15 August 2007 – the text says 2007. Area 123,600 ha = 68,000 ha land + 55,600 ha sea (Kyodo; Wikipedia gives 1,236 km² incl. 556 km² marine). The 2000 list of 15 land areas comes from a timorleste.tl page citing the UNTAET regulation (probably Regulation 2000/19) – check. Decree-Law 5/2016 (InforMEA/FAOLEX): its annex lists the protected areas (number not checked). Nino Konis Santana died in 1998 (earlier Lafa data). UNESCO: Tatoli (26 Aug 2025) says Timor-Leste is ready to register the park – check what was actually submitted. Tetun terms to check: ektare, ró-na'in, propoin, sanulu-resin-lima, uma-hospedajen.
- **Environmental challenges and conservation (tara bandu)** (`tl.nature.environment`, draft, 7 sources): Constitution Articles 61 and 139 are paraphrased from the official Tetun text (Konstituisaun RDTL) and the English translation (Constitute Project). Environmental Basic Law = Decree-Law 26/2012 (AMPEID). Tara bandu: suppressed during the occupation and revived after independence (Mongabay 2018, Coral Triangle Center); Vila Maumeta (Ataúro) tara bandu regulation of September 2017 (from search summary of CI/CTC material) – check. 'Most rural families cook with firewood' and 'burned during years of conflict' are general statements – check wording and figures against the 2022 census and the Chega! report. Ministry name (2026) from UNDP news. Tetun terms to check: meiu-ambiente, erozaun, Lei-Baze Ambiente nian, lia-na'in, lere no sunu (slash-and-burn), plástiku uza-dala-ida, efeitu-estufa, agrofloresta, lutu fatuk, rai mihis, uma-kreda.
- **Mammals of Timor-Leste** (`tl.nature.mammals`, draft, 7 sources): Species and IUCN categories follow the Wikipedia list derived from the IUCN Red List (seen via search): Acerodon mackloti VU, Pteropus griseus DD, P. vampyrus NT there (no category given in the text), Rusa timorensis VU, Phalanger orientalis LC. That list still shows Macaca fascicularis as LC, but the IUCN moved it to Endangered in 2022 (text uses EN) – check. Rhinolophus montanus: Endangered and endemic (Wikipedia, 'endemic to Timor-Leste'; text says 'only known from Timor'). 'P. vampyrus common around Dili' comes from a 2010 traveller's report (Mammal Watching) – anecdotal. The brief mentioned the 'common spotted cuscus', but the cuscus of Timor is the northern common cuscus (Phalanger orientalis); the text follows that. Rusa timorensis named from a Timor specimen (type locality) is from memory – check. Surviving native rats are hedged ('may still live'); the giant rats are extinct, timing not given (see tl.prehistory.ice_age_animals when written). Fruit-bat roles, echolocation, rat-borne disease and the advice not to handle bats or feed macaques are general knowledge. Tetun to check: niki-ferradura, laku (civet, as in tl.nature.animals), kuskus (the brief suggested 'meda'; no dictionary entry was found, so it is not used), rusa/bibi-rusa, lekirauk ikun-naruk, barullu, hada'et, toman, Ximpanzé/Orangotanu, and the Tetun names proposed for IUCN categories (Preokupasaun Menus, Vulneravel, Iha Perigu, Lakon Ona, Dadus La To'o). animalia.bio/timor-leste-animals could not be opened (fetching blocked) and did not appear in search results, so it is not cited. EN/ID (2026-11-11): translated from the Portuguese. Common names: large flying fox / kalong besar, Sunda flying fox / kalong sunda, Timorese horseshoe bat / kelelawar ladam timor, Timor deer / rusa timor (as in tl.nature.animals), long-tailed macaque / monyet ekor panjang, northern common cuscus / kuskus abu-abu, Asian palm civet / musang luwak; IUCN categories as in tl.nature.endangered. Check 'kalong sunda' and 'kuskus abu-abu': no single standard Indonesian name was found (Indonesian sources also call Phalanger orientalis 'kuskus timur').
- **Reptiles and amphibians** (`tl.nature.reptiles_amphibians`, draft, 7 sources): Counts come from search summaries of Kaiser et al. 2011 (ZooKeys 109: 263 specimens = 100 amphibians + 163 reptiles; pit vipers >25% of the snakes) and the 2015 review in Asian Herpetological Research (22 → more than 60 species, more than 20 undescribed, fieldwork 2011–2013, all districts except Oecusse) – check the figures and the team leader/authors against the papers. 'Timor python' (Malayopython timoriensis): two sources say it lives on Flores/Lembata and probably not on Timor; the brief asked for it, so the text explains the misleading name. Macklot's python (Liasis mackloti) in Timor-Leste is from memory (type locality Timor) – check against Kaiser's species list. Chelodina mccordi timorensis: Kuchling et al. 2007, Lake Ira Lalaro area; species Critically Endangered (IUCN) – check. Varanus timorensis on Timor and the 2013 Ataúro mention of a possibly undescribed monitor come from a search summary (the Ataúro paper itself was not identified) – check or remove. Teki = gecko (Lafa vocabulary); the tokay's presence is assumed from that. Sea snakes 'rarely bite' and the first-aid advice follow WHO guidance. Tetun to check: manduku (frog – not verified in a dictionary), sapu (toad; could be confused with sasapu 'broom'), girinu, erpetólogu, eskinku, varanu, pitaun, nanál fahe rua, ibun nia sorin mutin, kakorok-naruk, bee-lihun, kadeli, suu. EN/ID (2026-11-11): translated from the Portuguese. Names: osga = gecko / cicak; osga-tokay (teki) = tokay gecko / tokek (Lafa vocabulary: teki = tokek); escinco = skink / kadal; Varanus timorensis = Timor monitor / biawak timor; Malayopython timoriensis = Timor python / sanca timor (Mongabay Indonesia); Liasis mackloti = Macklot's python / sanca air (Indonesian name listed by GBIF); Trimeresurus insularis = white-lipped pit viper / viper bibir putih (GNFI); Chelodina = snake-necked turtle / kura-kura leher ular; lenuk = penyu (Lafa vocabulary). 'Oé-Cusse' is written Oé-Cusse Ambeno in en/id.
- **Whales, dolphins and dugongs** (`tl.nature.whales_dolphins`, draft, 9 sources): Season: timorleste.tl news (2025) gives October–December; its whale-watching page gives August–November for blue, sperm and pilot whales; a dive operator says mid-October to November – the text says 'mostly October–December'. CDU: southbound pygmy blue whale migration linked to monsoon, tides and upwelling; 2016–2023 study, at least 21 species (as in tl.nature.marine). 'More than 2,700 blue-whale sightings since 2014' is repeated from tl.nature.marine (ANU). Year-round dolphins, pilot and melon-headed whales: timorleste.tl. The Indonesian Throughflow passing the Ombai Strait, pygmy blue whales over 20 m, sperm-whale diving depths, baleen and toothed whale biology and dugong biology (about 3 m, long-lived, one calf every few years, IUCN Vulnerable) are general knowledge. No dugong sighting data for Timor-Leste were found, hence 'rare and little studied'. Regulation: Reccessary and eTurboNews (probably 2025) report no enforced rules or licences and a 2018 draft policy that was shelved; Blue Ventures lists a news item 'Timor-Leste announces whale-watching standard…' – its date and content were not seen, check. The good-practice list is generic (no distance in metres on purpose). Tetun to check: setáseu, baleia ho barba / ho nehan, kaxalote, baleia ulun-melaun, golfiñu-rotadór, sirénia, huu sai, maré, mergulla, “baka tasi nian”, operadór, lisensa, anunsia. EN/ID (2026-11-11): translated from the Portuguese; species names as in tl.nature.marine (pygmy blue whale / paus biru kerdil, sperm whale / paus sperma, short-finned pilot whale / paus pilot sirip pendek, melon-headed whale / paus kepala semangka, spinner dolphin / lumba-lumba spinner). 'O turismo nacional' is rendered 'the national tourism authority' / 'otoritas pariwisata nasional' (the source is timorleste.tl).
- **Insects, butterflies and other small animals** (`tl.nature.insects`, draft, 5 sources): Troides plato endemic to Timor (Wikipedia; a Timor endemic checklist on lntreasures.com also lists it). All Troides are in CITES Appendix II and Aristolochia vines are the host plants of birdwing caterpillars (general knowledge for the genus – check for T. plato). The Parantica 'Timor yellow tiger' and the endemic, rarely collected Delias come from search summaries (lntreasures checklist; Australian Museum blog) – species names left out on purpose. Ataúro honey (Apis dorsata, season just over a month, harvest usually in April, 2019–2021 French-led documentation) comes from ISIDORE records; two records appeared in search and the one cited should be confirmed. Beekeeping report (Southern Cross University, 2015) on A. mellifera and A. cerana. WHO malaria-free certification in 2025 as in symbols_society.json (not re-checked here). Dragonflies as clean-water indicators, orb-weaver spiders, crabs (hermit, ghost, fiddler; land crabs with sea larvae), scorpions and centipedes are general statements – no Timor-specific survey was found. Tetun to check: borboleta (no native word found), liras-manu, libélula, araña, ulat (caterpillar), ai-tali, bani boot, bani-been siin, kolleita, kaixa (hive box), kulambu (mosquito net), sentopeia, eskorpiaun, arakinídeu, térmita, besouru, hirus-matan (thorax), kadiuk-eremita/-fantasma/-violinista, entomólogu, kór-osan-mean. EN/ID (2026-11-11): translated from the Portuguese. Names: birdwing / kupu-kupu sayap burung; Apis dorsata = giant honey bee / lebah hutan; Apis cerana = Asian honey bee / lebah madu asia; stingless bees / lebah tanpa sengat; hermit, ghost and fiddler crabs / umang-umang, kepiting hantu, kepiting biola; dengue / demam berdarah (as in the quiz options already translated).
- **Endangered species and conservation status** (`tl.nature.endangered`, draft, 9 sources): IUCN categories come from general knowledge and Wikipedia (iucnredlist.org could not be opened): yellow-crested cockatoo CR; Timor green pigeon and Timor imperial pigeon EN (as in the tl.nature.birds notes); hawksbill CR; leatherback and olive ridley VU; dugong VU; blue whale EN; Rusa timorensis VU; Acerodon mackloti VU; Rhinolophus montanus EN; long-tailed macaque EN since 2022 (the Wikipedia list still shows LC); Chelodina mccordi CR. The green turtle is deliberately given no category: the IUCN may have moved it to Least Concern in 2025 – check and add if confirmed. 'Other Timor birds are Near Threatened' (e.g. Timor sparrow) – check. Lore IBA is named without claiming which species it holds. UNTAET Regulation 2000/19: in force 1 July 2000, protects endangered species and their habitats on land and at sea (bans killing, injuring, harming, taking or disturbing) and coral reefs (FAOLEX/UN text, via search); FAOLEX marks it as repealed – the text says later Timorese laws took its place (probably Decree-Law 5/2016; check). Joint Ministerial Order No. 18/MAP/MCIAII/2017 on protected aquatic species: contents not seen, so no species list is claimed. CITES: a UN SDG partnership page set a 2020 target for ratification; membership was not confirmed, so the text does not claim it. IUCN founded 1948, Red List since 1964 (general knowledge). Tetun names for IUCN categories are proposals for review: Preokupasaun Menus, Besik Ameasa, Vulneravel (stress rule; tl.nature.* elsewhere writes 'nível' with an accent), Iha Perigu, Iha Perigu Boot Tebes, Lakon iha Natureza, Lakon Ona, Dadus La To'o. Other Tetun to check: lenuk-pente, lenuk-kouru, lenuk-oliva (calques), despaxu ministeriál konjuntu, tama iha vigór, espésie akuátiku protejidu, Tratadu Lisboa. EN/ID (2026-11-11): translated from the Portuguese. IUCN categories: Least Concern / Risiko Rendah, Near Threatened / Hampir Terancam, Vulnerable / Rentan, Endangered / Terancam, Critically Endangered / Kritis, Extinct in the Wild / Punah di Alam Liar, Extinct / Punah, Data Deficient / Kurang Data; the group 'ameaçadas' (VU, EN, CR) = threatened / terancam punah. Indonesian Wikipedia uses 'Genting' for EN; Lafa uses 'Terancam' as the translation brief asks. Turtles: hawksbill / penyu sisik, leatherback / penyu belimbing, olive ridley / penyu lekang. 'Despacho ministerial conjunto' = joint ministerial order / keputusan bersama menteri.
- **Domestic animals and their role in culture** (`tl.nature.domestic_animals`, draft, 6 sources): Livestock numbers (chickens 928,806; pigs 419,169; cattle 221,767; goats 158,467; buffaloes 128,262; sheep 40,498) appear in a 2023 Jurnal Triton article; the pig figure matches the 2015 census used in tl.economy.livestock_fisheries, so the set is dated 2015 – confirm against the 2015 census agriculture report. 'More than 85% of households keep livestock (2010 and 2015 censuses)' comes from the same article. Horse numbers were not found. The 2019 Agriculture Census (INETL) has a livestock chapter, but its figures were not seen – add them when checked. ASF in Dili in September 2019 as in tl.economy.livestock_fisheries. Cultural roles (buffalo in barlake, funerals, kore-metan and uma lulik; horns kept in sacred houses or on graves in some regions; pigs and tais from the bride's side; chickens in small ceremonies) are general patterns that vary by region – a Timorese reviewer should check them, the horn custom in particular. Rabies: the text only says it is a threat 'in the region' (no Timor-Leste case data were checked) – update with the current situation; the first aid follows WHO. Muslim families and goat meat at feasts: general. Tetun to check: baka (cattle; the vocabulary has karau = buffalo and na'an-karau = beef), bibi-malae, uma-kain, kore-metan, hamulak, luto, respeitadu, ai-sunu, luhan, derepente, raiva, vasina, peste suína afrikana, isin-di'ak. EN/ID (2026-11-11): translated from the Portuguese; terms as in tl.nature.animals, tl.economy.* and tl.culture.* (buffalo / kerbau, cattle / sapi, cockfighting / sabung ayam, African swine fever / demam babi Afrika, sacred house / rumah adat).

## Prehistory (9)

- **The prehistory of Timor: from the sea floor to the first farmers** (`tl.prehistory.overview`, draft, 8 sources): Summary article: the details and their checks are in the other tl.prehistory.* articles and in tl.land.island_formation, tl.land.geology, tl.history.first_peoples, tl.history.austronesian_papuan and tl.history.sandalwood_trade. Ages are rounded (Permian about 299–252 Ma, Triassic 252–201 Ma; 'more than 250 million years' for the Permian sea floor). 'Bronze about 2,000 years ago or more' rests on the Baucau Dong Son drum (at least 2,000 years, press estimate). The timeline reuses the exact titles of tl.history.first_peoples (-44000) and tl.history.austronesian_papuan (-1500) and of other tl.prehistory articles, so the app's timeline merges them. Geological timeline dates are round numbers: -280000000 is a point inside the Permian (299–252 Ma), -208000000 is the 'over 208 million years' reported for the Lesululi ichthyosaurs, and -3000000 for Timor's emergence follows the 'about 3 million years' study mentioned in tl.land.island_formation (other studies differ). Period 'from' = start of the Permian. Tetun 'pre-istória' follows the category name in lafa/kb.py (Portuguese 'pré-história'). rezerva.tl/id/guide/history-guide and the inilah.com article were cited at the owner's request but could not be opened (fetching blocked) and did not appear in search results — check that they support the text (inilah: people in Timor-Leste 44,000 years ago). Tetun terms to check: pre-istória, paleontólogu, nehan-naruk (tusk), Institutu Jeosiénsia Timór-Leste nian, tambór bronze, espedisaun, ai-knanoik. EN/ID (2026-11-11): English and Indonesian translated by hand from the Portuguese and checked against the Tetun; Tetun and Portuguese not changed. Timeline titles shared between articles are translated identically. IGTL is written 'Timor-Leste Institute of Geosciences (Instituto de Geociências de Timor-Leste, IGTL)' / 'Institut Geosains Timor-Leste': the press uses several English names ('Timor-Leste Geoscience Institute', 'Institute of Geosciences') — check the institute's own. Dong Son drum = 'nekara' (id); Fernão de Magalhães = 'Ferdinand Magellan' (en) / 'Ferdinand Magelhaens' (id, as in tl.history.sandalwood_trade). Terms to check in Indonesian: ceruk batu (rock shelter), cap tangan (hand stencil), situs geologi, zaman Perm/Trias. The Tetun of 'Animál sira husi illa ida ne'ebé mesak' says 'tinan rihun barak' (many thousands of years) where the Portuguese says 'dezenas de milhares' (tens of thousands): the translations follow the Portuguese.
- **Fossils of the ancient seas: crinoids, ammonites and marine reptiles** (`tl.prehistory.fossils_seas`, draft, 9 sources): Crinoid, blastoid and ammonite biology is general palaeontology; Timor's Permian/Triassic setting follows the ANU 'Permian of Timor' and 'Triassic of Timor' records. From general knowledge, to check: 'enormous quantities… among the richest in the world', 'collected by early-20th-century European expeditions and now in museums' (e.g. Wanner's 'Paläontologie von Timor', the Dutch Timor expeditions), 'classic sites in West Timor' (e.g. Basleo), and Permian limestones of the Maubisse Formation with old volcanic rocks (as in tl.land.geology). The text avoids calling the Permian sea 'warm' (Timor's Permian latitude is debated). 1935 teeth: from the title of the Neues Jahrbuch paper ('Globidens timorensis E. von Huene 1935 not a durophagous mosasaur but an enigmatic Triassic ichthyosaur'); von Huene's first name and the year of the reinterpretation were not checked. Lesululi ichthyosaurs: found by an IGTL team in 2020 (Tatoli); ages reported as 'over 208 million years' (Tatoli, Apr 2026) and 'about 250 million years' (another report) — the text says 'more than 200 million'. Partners: Uppsala Museum of Evolution, University of Oslo Natural History Museum, University of Western Australia (Uppsala news, 14 Mar 2024). The owner-requested Badan Geologi page could not be opened and did not appear in search — check that it supports 'ichthyosaurs on Timor'. Use of fossils in oil and gas exploration (biostratigraphy) is general knowledge. Timeline: -280000000 is a round point inside the Permian (the classic Timor faunas span several Permian stages), -252000000 the end-Permian extinction (general knowledge), -208000000 the Lesululi ichthyosaurs ('over 208 million years', Tatoli). Tetun terms to check: Panjeia, superkontinente, fitun-tasi (sea star), lula, polvu, kuartu (chamber), barbatana (fin), hatolun (lay eggs), mozasauru, patrimóniu jeolójiku, jeoparke. EN/ID (2026-11-11): English and Indonesian translated by hand from the Portuguese and checked against the Tetun; Tetun and Portuguese not changed. Timeline titles shared between articles are translated identically. Terms (en/id): crinoid/krinoid, sea lily/lili laut, blastoid, ammonite/amonit, ichthyosaur/iktiosaurus, mosasaur/mosasaurus, Permian/zaman Perm, Triassic/zaman Trias, Cretaceous/zaman Kapur, Pangaea/Pangea, Maubisse Formation/Formasi Maubisse, UNESCO-recognised geopark/geopark yang diakui UNESCO. 'pé' of a crinoid is rendered 'stalk'/'tangkai'.
- **The pterosaur fossil (2026) and the Lesululi geosite** (`tl.prehistory.pterosaur_lesululi`, draft, 10 sources): All Lesululi facts come from Tatoli (11 Aug 2026 reports in Tetun, Portuguese and English — seen through search summaries; the owner-requested Indonesian report could not be opened) and earlier Tatoli reports; no peer-reviewed description exists yet. Place: Mount Lesululi, Cailaco administrative post, Bobonaro. Found in July 2026 during continued work on the ichthyosaur bones; reported 11 Aug 2026; identification provisional (IGTL president Job Brites dos Santos). Samples studied in Sweden, expected back for reconstruction and museum display. The English report names researchers from Uppsala University, the University of Oslo and the BBC; the Uppsala page (14 Mar 2024) adds the University of Western Australia and 2023 fieldwork. The BBC documentary planned for 2028 concerns the ichthyosaur fossil (Tatoli, Apr 2026). The pterosaur's AGE WAS NOT GIVEN — the text says so and gives the general pterosaur range (about 228–66 Ma). The article's 'period' (Triassic, 252–201 Ma) is the age reported for the Lesululi ichthyosaurs ('over 208 million years', Tatoli Apr 2026; 'about 250 million years', another report) — change it if the pterosaur proves younger. Geopark: candidacy prepared since 2025 (Tatoli pt, 2 May 2025; id/en, March 2025); Nino Konis Santana park named as a potential area (Tatoli tet, 29 Jul 2026); talks with Global Geoparks Network president Artur Sá and a community awareness event in Cailaco (search summaries). 'First pterosaur known from Timor-Leste' follows the English headline ('possible first pterosaur fossils'). Pterosaur biology (first flying vertebrates, fourth finger, wingspans up to about 10 m, not dinosaurs) is general knowledge. Timeline: -228000000 and -66000000 are the general first and last appearance of pterosaurs (range quoted in the Tatoli article); -208000000 is the reported age of the Lesululi ichthyosaurs, not of the pterosaur. Tetun terms to check: pterosauru, iktiosauru, jeosítiu, jeoparke, ruin-kotuk (backbone), liman-fuan dahaat, Muzeu Evolusaun, serbisu-kampu, sorumutu informasaun, amostra. EN/ID (2026-11-11): English and Indonesian translated by hand from the Portuguese and checked against the Tetun; Tetun and Portuguese not changed. Timeline titles shared between articles are translated identically. The Tatoli Tetun report (tatoli.tl, 11 Aug 2026) was opened on 2026-11-11: it confirms Mount Lesululi, Cailaco administrative post, Bobonaro; found in July 2026 during continued study of the ichthyosaur with Uppsala University, the University of Oslo and the BBC; provisional identification; samples sent to Sweden for study and reconstruction, then to come back for a museum; pterosaurs 228–66 million years ago; talks with GGN president Artur Sá. It names the IGTL president 'Job Brites' (no 'dos Santos') — check the full name. The English and Indonesian Tatoli pages could not be opened (host not reachable). Terms (en/id): pterosaur/pterosaurus, geosite/situs geologi (geosite), UNESCO Global Geopark/Geopark Global UNESCO, Global Geoparks Network/Jaringan Geopark Global, IGTL president/Presiden IGTL, National Electoral Commission/Komisi Pemilihan Nasional (as in politics_world.json).
- **Ice Age animals: Stegodon, giant rats and giant tortoises** (`tl.prehistory.ice_age_animals`, draft, 7 sources): Stegodon: Sartono 1969 (incomplete molar); U-series dates on a tusk from the Ainaro Gravels, at or before 130 ka (Louys, Price & O'Connor 2016, PeerJ). The find spot is given as West Timor because the paper is titled 'Timor Island, East Nusa Tenggara' — check. Extinction by slow demographic and genetic decline is the authors' suggestion. Giant tortoise: Megalochelys, Hooijer 1971; Timor record about 0.8–0.12 Ma (Wikipedia and Recently Extinct Species summaries); 'may be a distinct species'. Hooijer's first name (Dirk) from general knowledge. KOMODO-LIKE MONITOR ON TIMOR: not confirmed by my searches — from general knowledge (Hocknull et al. 2009, PLoS ONE, 'Dragon's Paradise Lost'); check or delete that sentence. Coryphomys: C. buehleri described 1937 (Schaub) from a cave near Nikiniki, West Timor; C. musseri Aplin & Helgen 2010 from East Timor archaeological deposits; about 5 kg (estimates vary; 6 kg is uncited); extinct roughly 2,000–1,000 years ago (Wikipedia). Louys et al. 2023 (Records of the Australian Museum 75(5)): few if any extinctions in 40,000+ years until the last few thousand; forest loss the main driver; no dated overlap with introductions (source title written descriptively). 'Bones next to food remains, so probably hunted' is an inference — check. Pleistocene 2.6 Ma–11,700 years and the island rule are general knowledge. Quiz 3 Tetun option uses 'grama' — check spelling. Timeline -2600000 = start of the Pleistocene (2.58 Ma, rounded). Tetun terms to check: Pleistosénu, regra illa nian, nehan-naruk (tusk), nehan molár, fatuk-oan (gravel), lagartu, dragaun Komodo, varanu Timór, laho endémiku, jéneru. EN/ID (2026-11-11): English and Indonesian translated by hand from the Portuguese and checked against the Tetun; Tetun and Portuguese not changed. Timeline titles shared between articles are translated identically. Checked 2026-11-11: the full text of Louys, Price & O'Connor 2016 (PeerJ, PMC4793331, seen through a search summary) places the dated tusk at the site of Wéaiwé near Atambua, West Timor, in the Ainaro Gravels (river-terrace deposits), so 'West Timor' is right. Terms (en/id): Ice Age/Zaman Es, Pleistocene/Pleistosen, Middle and Late Pleistocene/Pleistosen Tengah dan Akhir, island rule/aturan pulau, monitor lizard/biawak, Komodo dragon/komodo, Timor monitor/biawak timor, saltwater crocodile/buaya muara.
- **Crossing the sea: how the first peoples reached Timor** (`tl.prehistory.sea_crossing`, draft, 8 sources): Sea level about 120 m lower at the Last Glacial Maximum (about 21,000 years ago) and the end of the Ice Age at 11,700 years are general knowledge (consistent with tl.nature.biodiversity). Australia settled by at least 50,000 years ago (65,000 at Madjedbebe is debated) — general knowledge. Homo floresiensis ancestors on Flores about 1 million years ago — general knowledge; 'no clear evidence on Timor' (1960s claims of very old stone tools are not generally accepted) — check the wording. Laili: arrival about 44,000 years, an abrupt 'major migration', and 'the first settlers of Australia probably bypassed Timor' follow the UCL press release (May 2024) as summarised in search; 'perhaps by the northern route' is my inference — check. Smoke from fires as a sign of land beyond the horizon is a hypothesis in the literature, written as a possibility. Jerimalai figures repeat tl.history.first_peoples (Science 2011, Australian Geographic). Timeline -44000 and -42000 reuse the titles of tl.history.first_peoples on purpose (merged in the app's timeline). Tetun terms to check: estreitu, naturalista, rakit au, bero, ahi-suar, anin-boot, korrente, kail, redi, dalan norte/sul, Selebes, Neandertál, Australopiteku. EN/ID (2026-11-11): English and Indonesian translated by hand from the Portuguese and checked against the Tetun; Tetun and Portuguese not changed. Timeline titles shared between articles are translated identically. Terms (en/id): Ice Age/Zaman Es, glacial maximum/puncak glasial, rock shelter/ceruk batu, Borneo/Kalimantan, Celebes (Sulawesi)/Sulawesi (Celebes), Moluccas/Maluku, New Guinea/Nugini, Neanderthals/Neanderthal.
- **Archaeological caves: Jerimalai, Laili, Lene Hara and Matja Kuru** (`tl.prehistory.cave_sites`, draft, 8 sources): Dating methods (carbon-14 limit about 50,000 years; luminescence; uranium series) are general knowledge. Laili: 'layers below without human traces' simplifies the 2024 study (59,000–54,000-year layers); the diet of shellfish, fish and land animals such as rats follows the 2017 Laili study (Hawkins et al., 'broad-spectrum foraging'), recalled from memory — check. Jerimalai excavated by ANU teams led by Sue O'Connor (Science 2011). Lene Hara: shell beads and remains older than 30,000 years (Wikipedia mentions at least 35,000 years from shell beads — check). Matja Kuru 2: first used about 40,000 years ago; lakeside cave; Neolithic layers with pottery and domestic animals (SEAArch summary; Samper Carro et al.); dog about 3,000 years (2886–3068 cal BP, JCU paper). The location by Lake Ira Lalaro is from general knowledge — check. Glover 1966–67: Uai Bobo 1 and 2 near Venilale, Lie Siri and Bui Ceri Uato in today's Baucau municipality (search result: 'Bui Ceri Uato and the two Uai Bobo sites on the Baucau Plateau and near Venilale'); the finds are listed generally, giant-rat bones from his sites and the 1986 date of his monograph (Terra Australis 11) are from memory — check. Timeline -44000 and -42000 reuse the titles of tl.history.first_peoples on purpose. Tetun terms to check: abrigu fatuk, fatuk-didin, peneira, karvaun, luminesénsia, karbonu-14, estalaktite, kanutu/kontas (beads), matadalan, lia-na'in. EN/ID (2026-11-11): English and Indonesian translated by hand from the Portuguese and checked against the Tetun; Tetun and Portuguese not changed. Timeline titles shared between articles are translated identically. In 'Uai Bobo 1 and 2 (near Venilale), Lie Siri and Bui Ceri Uato' brackets were added in en/id to show which sites are near Venilale. The pre-filled en/id option 'Oecusse' (quiz question 3) was changed to 'Oé-Cusse Ambeno', the spelling of the style guide. Terms (en/id): rock shelter/ceruk batu, carbon-14/karbon-14, luminescence/luminesensi, shell beads/manik-manik dari cangkang kerang, Lake Ira Lalaro/Danau Ira Lalaro, lia-na'in kept in Tetun.
- **Rock art: images painted and carved in the caves** (`tl.prehistory.rock_art`, draft, 6 sources): Techniques (ochre, stencils made by blowing pigment, petroglyphs) and U-series minimum/maximum ages are general knowledge. Lene Hara faces: Antiquity 2010 ('Faces of the ancestors revealed… Pleistocene-age petroglyph'); age about 10,000–12,000 years or more, as in tl.history.first_peoples; 'among the oldest images of human faces known in the region' — check. The owner-requested National Geographic Indonesia article could not be opened (cited for the discovery story only). Hand stencils: at least 16, probably Pleistocene, older than the Austronesian Painting Tradition (Standish, García-Diez, O'Connor, Oliveira 2020; first names not given on purpose). Red pigment 29,000–24,000 years and visible paintings younger than about 6,300 years: U-series (Aubert et al. 2007; Oktaviana 2016 summary). Boats dominate the figurative motifs apart from small human figures, with high raked prows and steering oars (O'Connor 2003, as summarised in search); other motifs are kept general ('animals and other signs'). Socketed axes in Timorese rock art: Antiquity Dong Son paper (abstract). 'Ili Kére Kére is one of the richest painted shelters near Tutuala' is from general knowledge and place.ili_kere_kere — check. Period 'to' 1500 is open-ended (painting may have continued into recent centuries). Tetun terms to check: arte iha fatuk, petróglifu, liman nia marka (hand stencil), okre, pinsél, kamada minerál, remu (oar), baliun (axe), Tradisaun Pintura Austronézia, kosar (sweat), rai-rahun (dust). EN/ID (2026-11-11): English and Indonesian translated by hand from the Portuguese and checked against the Tetun; Tetun and Portuguese not changed. Timeline titles shared between articles are translated identically. Terms (en/id): rock art/seni cadas (as in place.ili_kere_kere), hand stencil/cap tangan, petroglyph/petroglif, ochre/oker, 'picotadas' = pecked/dipukul-pukul, socketed metal axe/kapak logam dengan lubang untuk tangkainya (kapak corong), Austronesian Painting Tradition/Tradisi Lukisan Austronesia, Metal Age/Zaman Logam.
- **The first farmers, pottery and domestic animals** (`tl.prehistory.first_farmers`, draft, 8 sources): Neolithic in Timor-Leste from about 3,500 years ago (ANU publication record) and pottery at least 3,500 years old (IRD record, Galipaud et al.). Out of Taiwan about 4,000–5,000 years ago and Austronesian arrival 4,500–3,000 years ago follow tl.history.austronesian_papuan. Dog: Matja Kuru 2, 2886–3068 cal BP, one of the earliest in the wider region (JCU paper). Pigs, macaques ('in the last few thousand years'), goats, deer and cuscus as human introductions follow tl.nature.animals and Louys et al. 2023 — their exact dates were not checked (one popular source says cuscus about 3,000 years; not used). Early crops (yams, taro, bananas) and shifting cultivation are hypotheses, written as 'it is thought / probably' — check with recent archaeobotany. Maize after 1500 from the Americas: general knowledge. Pigafetta's 1522 list (rice, bananas, sugar cane, buffaloes, pigs, goats) from memory of his account — check. 'Some villages still make pots by hand' — see tl.culture.crafts. 'Little is known about when pottery arrived' reflects a 2020 Journal of Indo-Pacific Archaeology paper on Early Metal Age pottery (search summary). Period: -2500 (Austronesian arrival) to about 1 CE (start of the Metal Age, approximate). Timeline -2500 and -1500 reuse the titles of tl.history.austronesian_papuan on purpose. Tetun terms to check: Neolítiku, modo-tahan, nono (boil), ai-han husi abut, talas, hudi, tohu, batar, bibi-rusa, kuskus, kole (tired soil), téknika. EN/ID (2026-11-11): English and Indonesian translated by hand from the Portuguese and checked against the Tetun; Tetun and Portuguese not changed. Timeline titles shared between articles are translated identically. Terms (en/id): Neolithic/Neolitikum, Island Southeast Asia/Asia Tenggara kepulauan, clay pots/periuk tanah liat, yam/ubi, taro/talas, long-tailed macaque/monyet ekor panjang, Timor deer/rusa timor, cuscus/kuskus, Metal Age/Zaman Logam. The Tetun of 'Viziñu foun, lian foun' says 'tinan rihun barak' (many thousands of years) where the Portuguese says 'dezenas de milhares' (tens of thousands): the translations follow the Portuguese.
- **Ancient trade and the first written records about Timor** (`tl.prehistory.early_records`, draft, 8 sources): Baucau Dong Son drum: found by chance at a construction site in late 2014; 1.03 m diameter, 78 cm high, about 80 kg; identified by Nuno Vasco Oliveira; at least 2,000 years old (Vietnamese press, e.g. Hanoi Times). The press says earlier, badly damaged drums had been found; the Antiquity paper (Oliveira, O'Connor, Bellwood) reports two drums from Timor-Leste — check the total number. Drum decoration (central star, birds, boats, feathered figures) and the name from a Vietnamese village are general knowledge. West Timor copper source and possible local copper/bronze working: Antiquity abstract. Zhao Rugua (maritime trade official, Quanzhou, 1225), Wang Dayuan (c. 1350) and the Nagarakretagama (Prapanca, 1365, for Hayam Wuruk) are general knowledge, consistent with tl.history.sandalwood_trade. PIGAFETTA DETAILS ARE FROM MEMORY of his account (Robertson translation), not confirmed by search: Victoria under Elcano at Timor 26 Jan–11 Feb 1522 (the crew's calendar was one day behind after crossing the Pacific); a chief held until buffaloes, goats and pigs were delivered; white sandalwood only there; ginger, rice, bananas, sugar cane, oranges, parrots; four kings at Oibich, Lichsana, Suai and Cabanaza; a junk from Luzon trading for sandalwood — check all. Links of Lichsana/Suai to Liquiçá/Suai are said to be debated. Tomé Pires (Suma Oriental, 1512–1515) wrote about Timor from Malacca without visiting (see tl.history.sandalwood_trade), hence 'first description by a European who was there' for Pigafetta. Period: about 1 CE (drums at least 2,000 years old) to 1522. Timeline 1225, 1350 and 1365 reuse the titles of tl.history.sandalwood_trade on purpose. Tetun terms to check: Illa Sunda Ki'ik, pulseira, kobre, estañu, junku, ai-lia (ginger), sabraka, kakatua, manu-fulun, lia-na'in, Quanzhou, hahi (praise). EN/ID (2026-11-11): English and Indonesian translated by hand from the Portuguese and checked against the Tetun; Tetun and Portuguese not changed. Timeline titles shared between articles are translated identically. Terms (en/id): Dong Son drum/nekara (genderang perunggu), socketed axe/kapak corong, Lesser Sunda Islands/Kepulauan Sunda Kecil, the poet Prapanca/pujangga Prapanca (Indonesian books often write 'Mpu Prapanca'), junk/jung, Magellan/Magelhaens. A 2026-11-11 web search did not find Pigafetta's passage on the four kings, so the Pigafetta details above are still unverified.

## History (20)

- **History of Timor-Leste: the eras** (`tl.history.overview`, draft, 7 sources): Overview summarises the 12 detailed articles of this file; facts for 1974–2025 follow the other writers' files (history_1974_2002, conflicts_recent, politics_world, world_recent): CAVR minimum 102,800; 78.5%; UN 191st member; maritime treaty signed 6 Mar 2018; WTO 30 Aug 2024; ASEAN 26 Oct 2025. Not verified this session: 'overseas province' in 1951. The tet Wikipedia title 'Istória Timór-Leste' is copied from the schema example — check it exists. Tetun terms: frente tolu, pelumenus, Komisaun lia-loos (CAVR), Organizasaun Mundiál Komérsiu, Fundu Petrolíferu, membru ba dala-191 / dala-11.
- **The first peoples (about 44,000 years ago)** (`tl.history.first_peoples`, draft, 7 sources): Check: Laili 'arrival signature' (~44,000 years; no clear signs 59,000–54,000) from ANU/UCL press releases of May 2024 — the phrase 'many stone tools and other traces appear' simplifies the study. Lene Hara inside Nino Konis Santana National Park; ochre fragment '>30,000 years' and 2020 hand stencils come from search summaries of the Wikipedia article. Rock-art subjects (people, boats, animals) are described for Tutuala caves in general. Tetun terms to check: fatuk-kuak (cave/rock shelter), fatuk-ahu (limestone), rai-kamada/kamada (layer), okre (rai-mean), 'sinál to'o mai' (arrival signature), métodu urániu, anzól, atun (tuna; Tetun 'tuna' = eel).
- **Austronesian and Papuan peoples** (`tl.history.austronesian_papuan`, draft, 7 sources): Austronesian arrival dates vary: 'by 4,500 BP' (Holton/Klamer/Kratochvil abstract) vs 3,000–4,000 years in other sources; 'at least 3,000 years side by side' from a 2019 Timor linguistics presentation. Pottery 'at least 3,500 years' (Galipaud and co-authors, 2014) is from a search summary — check the IRD record. Makasae 'about 102,000 (2010 census)' comes from the English Wikipedia article — confirm against census tables. Makuva 56 speakers (2010) from Tatoli; its language family is deliberately not stated (classification debated). Tetun terms to check: linguista, família lian, lian-inan, liafuan, knua, Timór loro-monu, Nova Giné, Madagaskar, ró-na'in.
- **Traditional kingdoms and the liurai** (`tl.history.kingdoms`, draft, 7 sources): Kingdom counts (Servião ~16, Belos ~46–50) are colonial-era estimates (NIU SEAsite, Wikipedia); Hägerdal (EIU paper, title written from memory — check) stresses that the two 'provinces' were colonial constructs. Check: 'dato' as noble title; liurai given 'Dom' and military ranks; 'rai' as Tetun word for a liurai's domain (from German Wikipedia via search). Wehali facts (Maromak Oan, attacks 1642 and 1665, centre on Dutch side after 1859) from English Wikipedia. Tetun terms: reinu, ukun-na'in, na'i-lulik, juramentu lulik, maun-boot, rai lulik (ritual domain), grau militár.
- **The sandalwood trade** (`tl.history.sandalwood_trade`, draft, 7 sources): Zhu Fan Zhi: '13th century' (timeline uses 1225, the usual completion date — check). Wang Dayuan c. 1350 (silver, iron, cups/goblets, coloured cloth) from a search summary of a study of Chinese sources. Nagarakretagama (1365) lists Timor among Majapahit's lands; the poem is not said to mention sandalwood. 1522 Magellan expedition and Luzon merchants from the Pre-colonial Timor article (search summary). 'From the 1550s to Macau' is a simplification. Makassar's 17th-century role is only lightly supported (Wehali's contacts with Makassar). Depletion 'by the 19th century' (Lundahl & Sjöholm; Barreto 2017) — no figures. Tree biology (root hemiparasite) from general knowledge — check. Quiz distractor tree names ai-teka (teak), ai-na, ai-bubur (eucalyptus) — check. Tetun: ai-isin (heartwood), insensu, osan-mutin, osan-mean, raru, Kompañia Olandeza Índia Orientál, Makasar.
- **1974: the Carnation Revolution and the first political parties** (`tl.history.carnation_parties_1974`, draft, 10 sources): Party founding dates (UDT 11 May, ASDT 20 May, APODETI 27 May, ASDT→FRETILIN 11 Sep 1974) from Wikipedia and the IRASEC chronology. Trabalhista (5 Sep 1974) and KOTA (founded Oct 1974 as APMT, renamed 20 Nov 1974) from Wikipedia infoboxes — check. Lemos Pires in office from Nov 1974 (Wikipedia: 8 Nov 1974). Macau talks (June 1975) and the Portuguese law of 17 July 1975 (Lei 7/75) are summarised loosely ('end Portuguese sovereignty within a few years') — check the exact timetable. UDT left the coalition on 27 May 1975 per IRASEC chronology (under pressure from Indonesian intelligence, BAKIN). 'Maubere' as FRETILIN's word for the rural poor: well known, not verified by search this session. Web search budget ran out during research; facts above were checked before that. Tetun terms: Revolusaun Kravu, ditadura, deskolonizasaun, ukun an rasik, koligasaun, ala radikál, kilat-ibun.
- **August 1975: the civil conflict** (`tl.history.civil_war_1975`, draft, 6 sources): Death toll 1,500–3,000 from Wikipedia (East Timorese civil war); Inside Indonesia suggests about 1,000 for Aug–Sep 1975 — check the CAVR/Chega! figure for the internal armed conflict. Governor's move to Ataúro: 26 Aug (Wikipedia 'Atauro') or 27 Aug (ETAN transcript of a CAVR hearing) — written as '26 or 27 August'. João Carrascalão as commander from the IRASEC chronology. FALINTIL founded 20 Aug 1975 from Portuguese-trained Timorese units, Nicolau Lobato among first commanders (Wikipedia 'Falintil'). UDT crossing into West Timor in Sept 1975 (Wikipedia: group of ~900 on 24 Sept); civilian refugee numbers left out on purpose. 'Leaders left Dili for the mountains' kept general (often said: Aileu). Tetun terms: funu-maluk, kontra-atake, funu-na'in, Timor Osidentál, ala radikál, golpe.
- **28 November 1975: the Proclamation of Independence** (`tl.history.proclamation_1975`, draft, 8 sources): Based on Wikipedia (DRET 1975, 1975 Council of Ministers) and the Constitution (preamble; Art. 1.2). Check: that Xavier do Amaral himself read the proclamation (well known, not verified by search this session); the exact place in Dili (often given as in front of the Government Palace) is left out. Council of Ministers took office 1 Dec 1975 (Wikipedia). 'Only a few countries recognised the RDTL' kept vague on purpose. Balibo Declaration 30 Nov 1975; the Wikipedia article on the Timorese Labour Party says it was signed in Bali. Balibo Five: 2007 NSW coroner (Dorelle Pinch); Roger East killed 8 Dec 1975. Borja da Costa killed 7 Dec 1975 (old Lafa data) — check. Nicolau Lobato killed 31 Dec 1978. Tetun: inu Pátria, asuwa'in nasionál, 'Balibó 5', Konsellu-Ministru, ministru negósiu estranjeiru.
- **From CNRM to CNRT and Indonesia's reformasi (1987–1998)** (`tl.history.cnrt_1998`, draft, 8 sources): CNRM date: the Constitution's preamble says 1987; Wikipedia gives 1986–1998 and CIDOB says it was constituted on 1 Jan 1988 after Xanana left FRETILIN in Dec 1987 — written as '1987 (some sources: 1988)'. Xanana captured 20 Nov 1992; life sentence 21 May 1993, later commuted to 20 years (ICJ/Amnesty; date of commutation not given). Peniche convention 23–27 Apr 1998 (Green Left, ETAN); Ramos-Horta one of two vice-presidents. 'Magna Carta' adopted at Peniche — from memory, check. Habibie: special autonomy offer 8 Jun 1998 (Wikipedia '1999 East Timorese crisis'); independence option 27 Jan 1999. Xanana moved to house arrest Feb 1999 (AFP via ETAN); freed in Sept 1999 (from memory). CNRT dissolved in June 2001 (UN daily briefing; another source says 9 June). Tetun: Konsellu Nasionál Rezisténsia Maubere/Timorense (Constitution spelling), reformasi, autonomia espesiál, dadur iha uma, Maubere.
- **The Nobel Peace Prize 1996** (`tl.history.nobel_1996`, draft, 7 sources): Prize facts from NobelPrize.org: shared in two equal parts, motivation quoted; announced in October 1996 (usually 11 Oct), presented in Oslo on 10 Dec 1996. Committee phrases ('foremost representative of the people of East Timor', 'leading international spokesman since 1975', 'oppression of a small people') are paraphrased — check against the press release. Belo: born 1948 (Wailakama, Vemasse, in today's Baucau municipality), Salesian, apostolic administrator of Dili from 1983, 1989 letter to UN Secretary-General Pérez de Cuéllar asking for a referendum — well known, not verified by search this session. 2022 sentence: on 29 Sept 2022 the Holy See press office confirmed restrictions imposed in Sept 2020 (modified Nov 2021) after accusations of sexual abuse of minors (CWR/NCR via CNA); kept to one neutral sentence as the brief asks, and the article is set to eb3+ for this reason. Ramos-Horta: born 1949 in Dili; President 2007–2012 and from 20 May 2022. Tetun: Prémiu Nobel ba Dame (dame = peace), amu-lulik saleziano, administradór apostóliku, ezíliu, porta-voz.
- **The 5 May Agreements and the Popular Consultation (1999)** (`tl.history.referendum_1999`, draft, 7 sources): Vote figures from the UN chronology and SG/SM/7119: 451,792 registered, 446,953 ballots cast, 344,580 (78.5%) against and 94,388 (21.5%) for special autonomy. Turnout is often quoted as 98.6% (446,953/451,792 = 98.9%), so no percentage is given in the text; the 98.6% in quiz Q2 is a distractor only. Result announced 4 Sept in Dili (3 Sept in New York). UNAMET: Res. 1246, 11 June 1999, unanimous; up to 280 civilian police and 50 military liaison officers; vote first scheduled for 8 Aug. Ian Martin as head, the 'three agreements' and Indonesian responsibility for security: well known, not verified by search this session. ~250,000 people taken or fled to West Timor (secondary source). Details of the violence left to tl.conflicts.violence_1999. Tetun: Konsulta Populár, boletin, votante rejistadu, autonomia espesiál, matan-okos (Constitution).
- **INTERFET: the international force (1999–2000)** (`tl.history.interfet`, draft, 6 sources): Res. 1264 adopted 15 Sept 1999 and its three tasks: well known (UN report S/2000/236 confirms the mandate), date not re-checked this session. 22 countries and about 12,600 personnel are Australian Defence figures (via Nautilus Institute), which 'varied during the deployment' — not a peak figure. Handover to UNTAET on 23 Feb 2000 (UN S/2000/236, Australian Senate inquiry); some sources say 28 Feb. UNTAET force commander Lt Gen Jaime de los Santos (Philippines). From memory, check: Habibie accepted a force on 12 Sept 1999; MPR accepted the result on 19–20 Oct 1999; last Indonesian troops left at the end of Oct 1999; Thai deputy commander; Japan's financial contribution; arrival in Oé-Cusse in Oct 1999. Tetun: forsa multinasionál, kapasete azúl, tulun umanitáriu, Asembleia Konsultiva Povu nian (MPR), vise-komandante.
- **UNTAET: the UN transition (1999–2002)** (`tl.history.untaet`, draft, 8 sources): Res. 1272 (25 Oct 1999). National Consultative Council created Dec 1999 by UNTAET Regulation 1999/2 (11 Timorese of 15 members). Regulation 2000/7 on the US dollar: signed 22 or 24 Jan 2000 (sources differ). Mixed cabinet in 2000 (usually dated July 2000) and the all-Timorese Second Transitional Government of Sept 2001 with Mari Alkatiri as Chief Minister (Reg. 2001/28; Constitution Art. 168). F-FDTL created Feb 2001. Special Panels for Serious Crimes (Constitution Art. 163: crimes of 1 Jan–25 Oct 1999); CAVR by Reg. 2001/10 (Art. 162). 2001 results: FRETILIN 55/88 (57.4%), PD 7, PSD 6, ASDT 6 (Wikipedia, UN press release). 2002 presidential result written as 'more than 80%' (usually given as 82.7%) — exact figure not verified by search. Annan's 'first time ... from referendum to statehood' paraphrased from UN coverage of 20 May 2002. Tetun: Administrasaun Tranzitória, regulamentu, Konsellu Konsultivu Nasionál, Ministru-Xefe, painél espesiál, timorizasaun.
- **20 May 2002: the Restoration of Independence** (`tl.history.restoration_2002`, draft, 8 sources): Ceremony on the night of 19–20 May 2002 on the outskirts of Dili: UN notes expected up to 200,000 people and delegations from at least 84 countries; eyewitnesses reported about 150,000; Megawati attended and was escorted by Xanana (UN News). Venue Tasitolu and the midnight flag change are well known but were not confirmed by search this session; other guests often named (Bill Clinton, John Howard, Jorge Sampaio) are left out because unverified. Constitution: Art. 167 (Constituent Assembly → National Parliament, 88 deputies), Art. 170 (in force 20 May 2002), preamble on recognition of the 1975 proclamation; Art. 13 official languages. UN membership: GA admission 27 Sept 2002 as 191st member (PGA statement). CPLP membership in 2002 from old Lafa data — exact date to check. 'First new sovereign state of the 21st century' — common description. Tetun: Restaurasaun Independénsia, kalan-boot, hola juramentu, órgaun soberania, membru ba dala 191.
- **Building the new State (2002–2006)** (`tl.history.state_building`, draft, 8 sources): Verified by search: UN membership 27 September 2002 (191st, CRS), F-FDTL established February 2001 (Wikipedia: 1 February 2001), full policing handed over 20 May 2004 (HRW 2006), Petroleum Fund created 2005 (month not checked; usually 3 August 2005, Law 9/2005). From the Tetun Constitution: Constituent Assembly elected 30 August 2001, approval 22 March 2002, in force 20 May 2002, 88 deputies in the first legislature, official and working languages. From general knowledge, not re-checked (search budget): Xanana elected April 2002; suco elections 2004–2005; Provedor working from 2005; Timor Sea Treaty 90/10 split signed 20 May 2002; US dollar since 2000 and centavo coins 2003; UNMISET to May 2005 then UNOTIL. Tetun to check: 'Tribunál Rekursu', 'polisiamentu', 'área dezenvolvimentu konjunta', 'moeda sentavu', 'membru da-191'.
- **UN missions in Timor-Leste (1999–2012)** (`tl.history.un_missions`, draft, 7 sources): Mission sequence and dates checked in search results (UN Peacekeeping, UNMIT site, Nautilus): UNAMET Jun–Oct 1999 (political), UNTAET Oct 1999–May 2002, UNMISET May 2002–May 2005 (two years plus a one-year extension), UNOTIL May 2005–Aug 2006 (political, no troops), UNMIT created 25 Aug 2006 by Resolution 1704, mandate completed 31 Dec 2012. From memory, to check: INTERFET authorised 15 Sep 1999 and landing 20 Sep 1999; UN PKF taking over from INTERFET in Feb 2000; local UNAMET staff among the 1999 victims; Japanese army engineers in UNMISET; first responders in 2006 (Australia, NZ, Malaysia, Portugal); ISF leaving in early 2013; UN country team agencies; Ian Martin as head of UNAMET (quiz). Details of the referendum, INTERFET and UNTAET are in their own articles; UN membership (2002) in tl.world.united_nations. Tetun: 'Administrasaun Tranzitória', 'Misaun Apoiu', 'Misaun Integrada', 'Koordenadór Rezidente', 'forsa manutensaun dame', 'família dezlokadu'.
- **The maritime boundary with Australia (2018)** (`tl.history.maritime_boundary`, draft, 8 sources): Checked in search results: treaty signed 6 Mar 2018 in New York, in force 30 Aug 2019 (CIL NUS, DFAT, Australian Foreign Minister); Greater Sunrise special regime and 70/30 or 80/20 split (DFAT, The Conversation); conciliation started 11 Apr 2016 as the first compulsory conciliation under UNCLOS, competence decision 19 Sep 2016, report 9 May 2018 (Kluwer, PCA documents); Greater Sunrise start delayed to ≈2034, Timor GAP agreeing to buy Osaka Gas's 10% (The Star, Sep 2026); Indonesia sea-border rounds Aug 2025–Sep 2026 (Tatoli). From memory, to check: Timor Gap Treaty Dec 1989; Timor Sea Treaty 20 May 2002 with 90% to Timor-Leste in the JPDA; CMATS Jan 2006 with an equal Sunrise split and a 50-year moratorium; CMATS ended in 2017 (notice in Jan, ceased in Apr — not confirmed in search); Copenhagen agreement on the central elements (Aug 2017); five-member commission with the PCA as registry; Morrison in Dili on 30 Aug 2019; '<400 nautical miles' between the coasts. Timor GAP's 2019 purchases: see tl.economy.petroleum. Spying case details: tl.world.australia. Tetun: 'liña mediana', 'plataforma kontinentál', 'milla náutika', 'konsiliasaun obrigatória', 'Área Konjunta Dezenvolvimentu Petrolíferu', 'rejime espesiál', 'kanu-gás', 'Haia', 'Kopenhaga'.
- **Timor-Leste 2012–2022** (`tl.history.decade_2012_2022`, draft, 11 sources): Checked in search results: first COVID-19 case 21 Mar 2020 and state of emergency in force from 28 Mar 2020 (Lowy, The Diplomat; Wikipedia's 2020 timeline dates the declaration 21 Mar); floods of 4 Apr 2021 linked to cyclone Seroja, 42 deaths counted by the UN around 7 Apr (other counts 27–46), 9,779 people in 18 evacuation centres in Dili, damage to the national medicine store holding vaccines (Vatican News/UN, UNFPA, Disasters Charter); Ataúro a separate municipality with effect from 1 Jan 2022 (Wikipedia; a Sept 2023 Council of Ministers proposal to turn it into a different first-level division was seen but its outcome was not — check); RAEOA/ZEESM (Law 3/2014), Alkatiri appointed in 2013, powers transferred Jan 2015, airport and mixed assessments (UNDP, La'o Hamutuk, Devpolicy); ASEAN application 2011 and observer status 2022 (Viet Nam News). Election and government details follow tl.politics.elections and tl.leaders.prime_ministers. From memory, to check: 'November 2022' (11 Nov, Phnom Penh), g7+ founded in Dili in 2010, ISF leaving in early 2013, vaccination starting in 2021, the President refusing to appoint some proposed ministers (2018–2020) and the coalition change in 2020. Tetun: 'pontu mudansa', 'Zona Espesiál Ekonomia Sosiál Merkadu', 'rai-halai' (landslide), 'armazén nasionál ai-moruk nian', 'estadu emerjénsia'.
- **Timor-Leste 2023–2026** (`tl.history.recent_2023_2026`, draft, 15 sources): Covers events up to October 2026 — later events need an update. Checked in search results: WTO 30 Aug 2024; Pope Francis 9–11 Sep 2024, ≈600,000 at Tasitolu on 10 Sep, 'first papal visit since independence' (Vatican News, UPI); Sept 2025 protests (from 15 Sep; 65 cars, ≈US$4m; purchase cancelled 16 Sep by Resolution 19/2025; vote to end lifetime pensions ≈26 Sep — its promulgation was not checked; crowd size >1,000 or >2,000 depending on source); ASEAN admission 26 Oct 2025 at the 47th Summit in Kuala Lumpur, first enlargement since 1999 (Malay Mail, Antara, Viet Nam News); first ASEAN meeting hosted in Dili 9–10 Jun 2026 and the Vietnam visit with visa-exemption agreement in June 2026 (US-ASEAN Business Council); amended 2026 budget ≈US$2.392bn approved 2 Jun 2026 and promulgated (Tatoli, La'o Hamutuk); Greater Sunrise start ≈2034 (The Star, Sep 2026); Indonesia sea-border rounds (Tatoli). Lú-Olo's death (21 Jun 2026, Kuala Lumpur, aged 71), 7 days of mourning and burial at Metinaro on 26 Jun: verified by the coordinator and leaders.json (Tatoli sources). Bayu-Undan's end in June 2025: Tatoli source used in tl.economy.petroleum (which gives 4 June 2025). 2023 election/IX Government details follow tl.politics.elections and tl.leaders.prime_ministers. Next elections in 2027 (presidential) and 2028 (parliamentary) are assumed from the five-year terms. Tetun: 'Simeira ASEAN', 'gás matan-been' (tear gas), 'pensaun ba moris tomak', 'Orsamentu Jerál Estadu retifikativu', 'luto nasionál', 'hanakonu', 'laran-susar'.
- **Archives and museums of the resistance** (`tl.history.archives_museums`, draft, 14 sources): AMRT: Wikipedia (via search) says its archive has collected internal resistance documents from individuals since 2002 and that the main exhibition is 'To Resist is to Win' (Resistir é Vencer); the opening date of the museum building was not found, so none is given. The Fundação Mário Soares / Casa Comum partnership is from memory – check before publishing. CAVR archive: about 7,700 statements as in tl.conflicts.reconciliation; hearings recorded on video and audio, and custody of the archive by the CNC (as successor of the post-CAVR secretariat), are from memory – check. ICRC documents 1974–1999 handed to the CNC (centrochega.gov.tl; handover date not seen). New archive at the CNC opened on 9 June 2026 (as in tl.conflicts.reconciliation). CAMSTL: Tatoli 2023 (set up by Stahl in Dili; cooperation with Coimbra and UNTL; footage gathered over about 25 years). UNESCO Memory of the World year: Wikipedia and Tatoli 2023 say 2013, another Tatoli article says 2003 and the Pacific Journalism Review 2012 – the text uses 2013; the register title 'On the Birth of a Nation: Turning Points' is from memory and kept only in the Portuguese text – check. Highest civilian honour in 2019 (Tatoli headline; the order's name is not given). Death in October 2021 (Asia Pacific Report, 29 Oct 2021). National Archive: permanent headquarters in Caicoli since August 2004 in a former INTERFET quarter; Decree-Law 4/2017 of 22 March (Wikipedia via search). National Library: government resolution in 2009, founding collection largely donated by the National Library of Portugal. Xanana Gusmão Reading Room: opened in 2000 (a June 2020 blog post marks its 20th anniversary), called the first public library after the 1999 referendum (Peace Run 2019), colonial-era villa, children's room, Xanana memorabilia (Lonely Planet, Dark Tourism); a cruise-line page says it is now called the Xanana Cultural Center and a 2024 visitor reported restoration paid for by Japan – check the current name. National Museum: no current information was found; only the old building in Comoro used by Arte Moris (see tl.culture.arts_literature) is mentioned. The photo blog is cited as a link only (no photos copied). Tetun to check: Rezisti mak Manán, Sentru Audiovizuál, Memória Mundu, kondekorasaun, Kruz Vermella, Biblioteka Nasionál, Kaikoli, lembransa, empresta, oráriu, Repórter imajen. EN/ID (2026-11-11): translated from the Portuguese. Names as in places.json and tl.conflicts.reconciliation: Archive and Museum of the Timorese Resistance / Arsip dan Museum Perlawanan Timor (AMRT); Centro Nacional Chega! kept in Portuguese; Max Stahl Audiovisual Centre Timor-Leste / Pusat Audiovisual Max Stahl Timor-Leste (CAMSTL); Universidade Nacional Timor Lorosa'e kept in Portuguese. 'Resistir é Vencer' is glossed 'To resist is to win' / 'Melawan berarti menang'. UNESCO Memory of the World = 'Ingatan Dunia' in Indonesian (check the usual Indonesian name of the register). 'Arquivo Nacional' = National Archives of Timor-Leste / Arsip Nasional Timor-Leste.

## Colonialism and occupation (10)

- **Arrival of the Portuguese (c. 1515) and the missions** (`tl.colonial.portuguese_arrival`, draft, 7 sources): 1515 (some sources: 18 August 1515) is weakly sourced — English Wikipedia flags the 1512–1515 claim as needing a citation; Timor on a Portuguese map in 1512 (Lifau article). Solor fort: 1560s in some sources, 1571 in others. 'Baptised some rulers' and 'sailed with the monsoon' are general statements. Monument details (bronze caravel ~13 m, figures, 1974 padrão nearby, inaugurated 27 Nov 2015) from Wikipedia, government press material and German wiki via search. Tetun: frade dominikanu, vise-rei, kapela, ró-vela, padraun (marku fatuk), Komunidade Nasaun sira ne'ebé ko'alia Portugés (CPLP).
- **The Topasses and the 17th–18th centuries** (`tl.colonial.topasses`, draft, 7 sources): Check: origin of the name 'Topass' (Indian word, debated) — not verified this session; Jan de Hornay as a Dutch deserter and António Hornay as his son; Mateus da Costa as a leader; the title capitão-mor. VOC base at Kupang 'in the 1650s' (often given as 1653) not verified this session. Penfui details (9 Nov 1749; allies withdrew; Gaspar da Costa killed) from English Wikipedia; casualty figures left out because sources disagree (2,000+ vs 40,000). The Pereira source title is quoted from a truncated search result. Tetun: Topasse, 'Portugés metan', kapitaun-mór, serka (besiege), kilat, funu-fatin, aliadu.
- **From Lifau to Dili (1702–1769)** (`tl.colonial.lifau_to_dili`, draft, 8 sources): Guerreiro's term: 1702–1705 (Wikipedia list) or 1702–1704 (World Statesmen); 'driven out after a few years' (Topasses article). '40 or 50 houses' is quoted from an RTP headline; the original author of the phrase was not seen. 1769: besieged by forces of Francisco Hornay (one Wikipedia article calls him a liurai, Francisco d'Horney); 11 Aug 1769 (Lifau destroyed) and 10 Oct 1769 (work on Dili begins) from History of Dili and the Teles de Meneses article; ~1,200 people from the Bay of Dili article; Vemasse as first choice from the Dili article. Wallace's 1861 visit and his poor opinion of Dili are written from memory of 'The Malay Archipelago' — check. Tetun: kapitaun-jenerál, sede, serka/serku, baía, illa Sunda Ki'ik, naturalista.
- **Dutch rivalry and the partition of Timor (1859–1916)** (`tl.colonial.dutch_partition`, draft, 7 sources): 1916: no separate 1916 instrument was found; secondary sources say the division was 'settled in 1916' or 'concluded in 1916–17', while the award is dated 25 June 1914 — text says 'completed around 1916–1917'. 1904 convention date (1 Oct 1904) from a PCA document via search. Maucatar to Portugal and Noimuti to the Netherlands (1904) come from a search summary; one snippet of the 1914 award mentions Noimuti together with the Oecusse enclave — check. Lardy's Swiss nationality — check. Treaty of Lisbon exchange (Flores, Solor, Adonara vs Maubara, claims to Ambeno, Ataúro) and Maubara handover in 1861 from Wikipedia. 2005 land-border agreement from the old Lafa atlas; some segments disputed until at least 2019. Tetun: Tratadu Lisboa, ratifika, reivindikasaun, rai-le'un, Tribunál Permanente Arbitrajen nian, Haia, jurista Suisa, árbitru, segmentu.
- **Colonial rule, taxes and the coffee economy** (`tl.colonial.colonial_rule`, draft, 7 sources): Head tax: no source found for its start year (often said to be 1906–1908) — text says 'early 1900s'; 'on each adult man' and 'those who could not pay had to work' are general descriptions to check. Coffee at Maubara in 1815 'introduced by the Dutch' and Castro's Java-style plan come from English Wikipedia (Agriculture in Timor-Leste); Castro's term is 1858 or 1859–1863 in different sources. Remexio post (Aug 1894) with coffee nursery and the ~70-soldier Dili garrison come from a BNU/CGD study via search. 'Main export by the late 19th century' is general. Tetun: pasifikasaun, postu militár, viveiru, impostu ulun, tributu, kobra, borraxa, kopra, 'estadu ida iha estadu nia laran'.
- **Revolts and the Manufahi war (1911–1912)** (`tl.colonial.revolts_manufahi`, draft, 6 sources): Earlier revolts (1894–1901, 1907–1908) are attributed to Dom Boaventura in the English Wikipedia rebellion article — some histories name other Manufahi leaders for the 1890s; check. Rebels '12,000+' and Portuguese-record casualties (3,424 rebels dead, 12,567 wounded; 289 dead on the Portuguese side) from the Wikipedia infobox; Timorese estimates 15,000–25,000 from the Same article. A massacre at Dai-Sua (Aug 1912, ~3,000 people) reported in the Same article was left out pending verification. Capture at Betano and death on Ataúro (Same article) vs a mountain-top siege and breakout (other account): text keeps his fate uncertain. Filomeno da Câmara as governor (1911–1913) — check. Order of Dom Boaventura (2007) from a Portuguese academic article via search. Tetun: rebelde, ró-funu, kanek, prizioneiru, Orden Dom Boaventura, lenda, funu-na'in fundadór, railiur (as in Constitution Art. 11).
- **World War II and the Japanese occupation (1942–1945)** (`tl.colonial.ww2`, draft, 6 sources): Timorese deaths: 40,000–70,000 (Wikipedia infobox) vs 40,000–60,000 (other sources); 'at least 40,000' is the common floor — text gives the range and says sources disagree. From general knowledge, to check against Chega! Part 3 or the AWM: Portugal's protest at the Dec 1941 landing; the 2/4th Independent Company joining in 1942; the Japanese-backed 'black columns' (colunas negras); confinement of Portuguese residents. Surrender signed 11 Sep 1945 aboard HMAS Moresby (NAA); Dili ceremony 24 Sep 1945 with Governor Manuel de Abreu Ferreira de Carvalho (WA Museum). Tetun: Funu Mundiál Daruak, Aliadu sira, neutru/neutralidade, guarnisaun, kalan-balu, gerilla, patrulla, kriadu, 'koluna metan', rende-an/rendisaun.
- **Portuguese Timor 1945–1974 and the 1959 Viqueque revolt** (`tl.colonial.postwar`, draft, 7 sources): From general knowledge, not verified this session: 'overseas province' status in 1951 and the 1960 UN General Assembly declaration (Res. 1542 of Dec 1960); division into concelhos and administrative posts; post-war rebuilding with 'often unpaid or forced' labour (check Chega! Part 3). Viqueque 1959 (7–14 June; Uatolari, Uatucarbau; Permesta exiles; Dili-administration plotters; leaders deported to Angola and Portugal) from Wikipedia and G. Gunn's draft; 'hundreds of deaths' only in some accounts; the exiles' arrival in 1958 — check. Education figures: ~90% illiteracy (1975) from the UNESCO EFA review; >94,000 primary pupils (1974) via the IEJ review; no university until the mid-1980s. Tetun: provínsia ultramarina, diktadura, Estado Novo, konsellu, postu administrativu, ukun-an, Revolusaun Kravu, katekista, deskolonizasaun, kbiit.
- **7 December 1975: the Indonesian invasion and annexation** (`tl.colonial.indonesian_invasion`, draft, 11 sources): Invasion details from Wikipedia (Indonesian invasion; Battle of Dili): naval bombardment, paratroopers and marines on 7 Dec 1975; Baucau 10 Dec. Many civilians killed in Dili in the first days (including members of the Chinese community) — only Roger East is named. Provisional Government 17 Dec 1975 (Araújo/Lopes da Cruz). 'People's Assembly' of 37 members in May 1976 (usually dated 31 May 1976). Annexation law passed 16 July, signed 17 July 1976 (Australian Senate report); the Wikipedia 'East Timor (province)' infobox says 17 May — conflicting. UNGA 3485 (12 Dec 1975, 72–10–43) described neutrally as 'criticised ... called for withdrawal'. UNSC 384 unanimous; 389 adopted 12–0 with Japan and the US abstaining (Benin did not vote). 'A GA resolution every year 1975–1982' from memory — check. CAVR minimum 102,800 deaths. Tetun name of Memorial Day ('Loron Memória') — align with the calendar writer. Tetun: ró-funu, parakedista, soldadu marina, autodeterminasaun, podér administrante, Funu Malirin, tasi-okos.
- **Life under Indonesian occupation (1976–1999)** (`tl.colonial.indonesian_occupation`, draft, 10 sources): 13 regencies (kabupaten) from memory — check. Governors Araújo, Mário Carrascalão, Abílio Osório Soares: names verified, terms not given. 'Indonesia built many schools; more children went to school than in colonial times' — widely reported but not verified by search this session (check Chega! Part 7.9). Portuguese banned (Wikipedia 'East Timorese Portuguese'); Externato São José reportedly the last Portuguese school, closed 1992. Catholics 25–30% (1975) → over 90% (1990s) (Hodge 2013, via search). Transmigration from HRW 1997; Dec 1998 announcement by Governor Abílio Soares. Coffee trade controlled by military-linked companies (e.g. P.T. Denok) — from memory, check. Province declared 'open' on 1 Jan 1989 (TimorLink, Australian Left Review), but access stayed restricted. CAVR minimum 102,800 deaths (≈18,600 killings/disappearances, ≈84,200 hunger and illness). Pope's Mass at Tasitolu 12 Oct 1989 with pro-independence banners (Vatican News 2019). Massacres left to tl.conflicts.* articles. Tetun: kabupaten, sub-distritu, suku, transmigrasaun, la'o-la'ok, frente klandestina, baluk-haat ida.

## Conflict and reconciliation (9)

- **Conflicts in Timor-Leste's history: an overview** (`tl.conflicts.overview`, draft, 5 sources): Overview linking the conflict articles. Check: Manufahi war details (troops from Mozambique, 'thousands killed') and WWII toll ('tens of thousands'; Wikipedia gives about 40,000–70,000) are from general knowledge; the web-search budget ran out before they could be re-checked, so compare with the tl.colonial.revolts_manufahi and tl.colonial.ww2 articles. CAVR figures: minimum 102,800 (HRDAG gives ±11,000; a CAVR-related memo ±12,000), upper estimate 183,000, 18,600 killed/disappeared. Displaced in 2006: about 150,000. Tetun terms to check: 'kampaña sirku no aniquilasaun' (encirclement and annihilation), 'ema dezlokadu', 'kilat boot' for artillery.
- **The resistance: armed, clandestine and diplomatic fronts** (`tl.conflicts.resistance_fronts`, draft, 7 sources): Konis Santana took over FALINTIL's command after Ma'huno's arrest (April 1993) and died on 11 March 1998 (Wikipedia; place and cause of death differ between sources, so they are left out). Three fronts, CNRM (1987) and CNRT (1998) follow the Tetun Constitution preamble; some sources give 1988 for CNRM. From general knowledge, not re-checked because the web-search budget ran out: the 1981 national conference (usually dated March 1981 and placed in Lacluta, Viqueque; written here only as '1981'); FALINTIL made non-party in 1987; life sentence reduced to 20 years; Xanana freed September 1999. Tetun terms to check: 'baze de apoiu', 'kantonamentu', 'gerrilleiru', 'rai-li'ur'.
